THE 


DURHAM  HOUSEHOLD  BOOK; 


OR, 


THE  ACCOUNTS 


OF  THE 


BURSAK  OF  THE  MONASTERY  OF  DURHAM. 


FROM  PENTECOST  1530  TO  PENTECOST  1534. 


LONDON: 

J.  B.  NICHOLS  AND  SON,  PARLIAMENT  STREET ; 
WILLIAM  PICKERING,  PICCADILLY ; 

EDINBURGH  : 
LAING  AND  FORBES. 


AT  a  MEETING  of  the  COUNCIL  of  the  SURTEES  SOCIETY 
on  the  6th  day  of  September,  1844, 

RESOLVED,  That  the  BURSAR'S  BOOK  IN  THE  EXCHEQUER 
OF  DURHAM  be  one  of  the  SURTEES  SOCIETY'S  Publications 
for  the  year  1844,  and  that  the  SECRETARY  be  requested  to 
edit  the  same  on  the  usual  terms. 

JAMES  RAINE,  Secretary. 


PREFACE. 


THIS  volume  consists  of  an  accurate  transcript  of 
the  original  private  Account  Book  of  Robert  Benett, 
Bursar  of  the  Priory  of  Durham  for  the  period  of  four 
years,  from  Whitsuntide  1530,  to  Whitsuntide  1534. 
One  gathering  of  paper  in  the  first  year,  consisting 
apparently  of  about  twenty  leaves,  is  unfortunately  want- 
ing. The  book  is  of  a  small  quarto  size,  in  a  wrapper 
of  parchment  without  boards,  and  is  preserved  in  the 
office  of  the  auditor  of  the  Bishop  of  Durham.* 

Other  books  of  the  same  character,  more  or  less  per- 
fect, are  in  the  custody  of  the  Dean  and  Chapter  of 
Durham  ;  but,  as  they  contain  more  of  receipt  than 
expenditure,  each  book  being  preceded  by  a  rental, 
which  is  the  same  in  every  year,  and  few  of  them  enter- 
ing into  much  detail  with  respect  to  purchases  or  pay- 

*  In  the  book  is  a  loose  letter-back,  containing  references  to  some  of 
its  contents,  in  the  handwriting  of  the  person  to  whom  it  is  addressed, — 
"  To  his  woorthy  freinde  Mr  John  Richardson,  at  his  house  in  Durham, 
these  dd."  Now,  as  John  Richardson  held  an  office  in  the  Exchequer, 
under  the  Bishop  of  Durham,  in  the  earlier  part  of  the  seventeenth  cen- 
tury, it  is  probable  that  the  book  has  been  preserved  in  its  present  place 
of  custody  at  least  since  his  period. 


PREFACE. 

ments,  they  are  not  of  so  interesting  a  nature  as  that 
which  is  now  presented  to  the  members  of  the  Society. 
They  have,  however,  as  will  be  seen  from  the  Glossary 
appended  to  these  pages,,  afforded  many  explanations  of 
terms  used  in  the  present  book,  and  many  very  valu- 
able illustrations. 

The  nature  of  the  book  must  next  be  described.  The 
Bursar  was  the  chief  officer  of  the  monastery,  under  the 
Prior,  having  under  him  all  the  other  officers  (obedien- 
tiarii)  of  the  house,  each  of  whom  exhibited  to  him 
yearly,  at  Pentecost,  an  account  of  his  receipts  and 
expenses  in  his  peculiar  office.  To  the  sacrist,  for 
instance,  were  appropriated  certain  tithes  and  revenues 
to  defray  the  expenses  incidental  to  the  performance  of 
divine  service  in  the  church,  the  purchase  of  robes,  and 
the  repairs  of  the  fabric.  Every  other  department  had 
its  respective  head,  but  all  accounted  to  the  Bursar;* 
and,  when  their  various  yearly  account  rolls  had  been 
laid  before  him,  and  had  been  duly  examined  and 
audited,  one  large  roll  was  yearly  compiled,  called  the 

*  "  The  Bowcer's  office  was  to  receave  all  the  rentes  that  was  per- 
teyning  to  the  house,  and  all  other  officers  of  the  house  mayde  there 
accoumptes  to  him,  and  he  discharged  all  the  servants'  wages,  and  paid 
all  the  expences  and  sommes  of  money  as  was  laid  furth  about  any  work 
apperteining  to  the  said  Abey,  or  that  the  house  was  charged  with  all." 
— The  Monastical  Church  of  Durham,  p.  83. 


PREFACE.  IX 

Bursar's  Roll,  embodying  the  whole  proceedings  of  the 
monastery  in  a  summary  way,  and  was,  with  the  rolls 
of  the  inferior  officers  upon  which  it  was  founded,  duly 
deposited  in  the  treasury  or  muniment  room  of  the 
convent.  The  Bursar's  Books  here  printed  contain  the 
most  minute  details  of  his  own  proceedings  as  Bursar 
during  their  respective  years,  and  their  substance  was 
duly  transferred  to  his  great  annual  account  roll — but 
their  substance  only.  In  the  roll  he  contents  himself 
with  stating  in  general  terms  his  expenditure  under  its 
respective  heads  ;  but  here  in  his  book  we  have  daily 
memoranda  under  every  department  within  his  care, 
with  the  most  minute  specification  of  names  and  prices, 
together  with  numerous  other  entries  to  aid  his  me- 
mory and  guide  him  in  his  proceedings.  In  addition 
to  much  voluntary  and  valuable  information  of  this 
nature,  the  fly-leaves  at  the  beginning  and  end  of 
each  year  abound  with  the  most  curious  entries,  which 
the  Bursar  intended  for  no  eye  save  his  own  ;  but  these 
entries  do  not  constitute  the  chief  value  of  his  book. 
We  have  here  every  article  of  food  and  clothing,  the 
whole  consumption  in  short  of  the  great  monastery  of 
Durham  for  four  years,  most  minutely  detailed.  Men 
and  their  wages,  prices  and  quantities,  necessaries  and 
luxuries,  are  all  set  forth  to  the  inquisitive  reader  by  a 

b 


X  PREFACE. 

contemporary  pen ;  and  to  aid  him  in  his  inquiries, — 
more  than  this  the  Society  cannot  do.  in  conformity 
with  its  plans. — an  abstract  of  the  various  matters  and 
things  specified  in  our  book,  and  a  short  glossary  of 
such  words  as  seemed  to  require  explanation,  together 
with  copious  indexes  of  men  and  places,  are  printed  as 
an  Appendix. 

J.  R.

EXPLANATION  OF  WORDS. 


A, 


ALLEGES  ALBI.  Dried  herrings,  retain- 
ing their  white  colour.  "  1539-40.  Paid 
to  William  Blythman  and  Ralph  Bow- 
man for  the  carriage  of  1000  white 
herrings,  and  two  loads  of  '  poudcrt  ' 
fish  with  four  horses,  at  6V.  each,  2*." 
— Bursar's  Book. 

ALLEGES  SORI.  Dried  herrings  of  a  red 
colour.  The  "  poudert  "  fish,  or  fish 
red  with  salt,  mentioned  under  the 
preceding  word.  See  SORUS. 
ALMAYNE  REYVETTS,  139.  Defensive  suits 
of  armour  for  the  body,  composed 
of  splints  rivetted  together.  Kempe. 
They  are  here  called  Almayne  or  Ger- 
man, perhaps  from  the  country  in  which 
they  were  chiefly  used,  or  from  which 
they  were  introduced  into  England. 
AMPNUS  or  UM.  A  corruption  of  amnis, 
a  river.  Repanicio  Ampni,  p.  280, 
iScc.  the  repairing  of  the  mill  dam  at  the 
west  end  of  the  cathedral.  The  dam 
was  made  of  .stone,  and  the  crevices 
were  stuffed  with  moss,  of  which  large 
quantities  were  procured  every  year 
for  this  purpose.  It  was  generally 
gathered  and  brought  to  the  dam  by 
girls. 

ANGELL  NOBLES,  325.     The  gold  coin  or 
noble,  so  called  from  the  angel  repre- 
sented on  one  of  its  sides. 
ANNES.     Anniseeds. 

APPRENTICIUS.  An  apprentice.  "  Received 
of  the  Sacrist  for  the  table  (or  board) 
of  the  apprentice  of  the  Lord  Prior, 
265.  8d." — Bursar's  Book  tor  1517. 
AQUA  DULCIS.  Fresh  water,  in  opposi- 
tion to  salt  water  or  the  sea.  Pisces 
rt^uor  dulcis,  fish  from  rivers  or  brooks. 
The  Brownie  is  frequently  called  "aqua 
dulcis  de  Brun." 


ARIES,  p.  29,  &c.  Apparently  used  for 
sheep  in  general. 

ARTII,  p.  182.     The  hearth. 

ASCENDENDO,  90,  &c.  This  word  mu»t 
be  taken  in  connection  with  DESCEX- 
DENDO,  its  opposite,  terms  whicli  are 
in  this  book  applied  to  the  wages  of  the 
priests  officiating  in  the  chantries  of 
Bishop  Hatfield,  the  two  Nevilles,  and 
Robert  Rodes,  within  the  cathedral. 
The  Bursar's  Book  for  1508  affords  an 
explanation  of  the  distinction  and  of 
the  meaning  of  each  word.  "  Descen- 
dendo  ad  inferiorem  *  is  the  entry  in 
the  book  for  that  year.  It  would  there- 
fore appear  that  when  the  word  descen- 
dendo  is  used,  the  sen-ice  at  the  begin- 
ning of  the  year,  immediately  after 
Whitsuntide,  was  undertaken  by  a 
monk  of  superior  standing,  who  was 
succeeded,  month  after  month,  by  his 
juniors  in  their  respective  order  ;  and 
that  the  term  ascendendo  implies  the 
converse  of  this  arrangement,  the 
junior  monk  beginning  and  giving 
place  to  his  immediate  senior,  who 
passed  the  duty  on  to  the  man  above 
him,  and  so  on,  till  the  end  of  the 
year. 

ASSERES,  85.  Spars  of  wood  for  the  roof 
of  a  house,  or  perhaps  occasionally 
laths.  As  they  were  obtained  from  the 
log  of  timber,  not  by  sawing  (sar- 
racio),  but  by  tearing  or  splitting 
(laceratio),  it  is  clear  that  boards 
cannot  bo  meant  when  this  word  is 
used. 

AVENARIUS,  328.  The  keeper  of  the 
oats. 

A  CCA,  233.  Notwithstanding  the  mo- 
dern usage  of  the  word,  as  exclusively 
applicable  to  the  puffin,  it  is  clear  that 
awca  is  here  used  for  a  goose. 


334 


EXPLANATION   OP   WORDS. 


li. 


BAGMAN.  An  officer  of  the  Convent,  but 
his  peculiar  duties  are  not  apparent. 

BAKGEYR,  139.  Armour  for  the  back  in 
opposition  to  Forgeyr,  or  armour  for 
the  front. 

BANDSMEN.  Binders  of  the  sheaves  after 
the  reapers  in  the  harvest  field,  See 
YANE. 

BAPTIZACIO,  142.  We  may  here  give  the 
expenses  at  another  christening. "  1539- 
40.  In  the  expenses  of  the  Lord  Prior 
and  his  attendants  at  Brauncepeath  in 
Advent,  at  the  baptizing  of  the  daugh- 
ter of  the  Earl  of  Westmerlande,  2s.  4<Z. 
In  regards  (presents)  given  at  the  bap- 
tizing of  the  daughter  of  the  Earl  of 
Westmerlande  at  Brauncepeath,  10. 
Dec.  20^." — Bursars  Boot:. 

BARKED,  29.  Tanned,  by  means  of 
bark. 

BASERS,  LE,  95,  &c.  No  one  of  the  Bur- 
sar's books  explains  this  word.  The 
Valectus  Promptuarii,  in  connection 
with  whom  it  occurs,  is  in  the  book 
for  1517,  called  the  Butler.  The  later* 
were  perhaps  the  lower  tables.  "  1517. 
To  Adomar  Dand,  '  butler,'  20s.  Item, 
6s.  8d.  for  '  baser.'  Whereof  paid  in 
* Segisterio,"1  4d. "  &c.  See  SEGIS- 

TERIUM. 

BEDNELL,  Beydnell,  codlynge.  Codlings 
or  codfish  of  a  smaller  size  from  Bead- 
nell  in  Northumberland. 

BIEBMAN,  329.  The  servant  in  charge 
of  the  cow  byers. 

BLANDCORN,  262.  This  word  occurs  un- 
der the  head  of  thrashing  and  win- 
nowing of  tithe  corn  at  Southwick. 
Wheat,  barley,  oats,  pease,  and  beans 
are  all  mentioned  by  name.  Bland- 
corn  is  no  doubt  maslin  (mixtilio)  from 
Sax.  blendan,  to  mix. 

BODITRACES,  19,  &c.     Horse-trappings. 

BOLCORN,  5,  &c.  Qu.  corn  for  bulls. 
But  perhaps  this  is  a  term  for  some 
old  service  payable  to  the  lord  by  his 
tenant. 

BORDCLOTII.  A  table  cloth.  «  Two 
'  bordcloth,  elbroyde,'  (of  the  breadth 
of  an  ell)  for  the  Prior's  table,  contain- 
ing 14  ells.  One  tabylcloth  8  ells  in 
length,  and  1$  in  breadth."  Bursar's 
Book  for  1539-40.  In  the  same  book 
lord  clothes  are  called  over  clothes.

ELEEMOSINA,   55.     The  entry  under  this 
word,   in  the  page  referred  to,  is  to  be 
distinguished  from  DISTRIBUCIO  above, 
inasmuch  as  the  money  given  as  alms 
arose  from  a  specific  foundation  for  that 
purpose.     The  entry  under  this  head 
in  the  Book  for  1536-7,  sets  out  these 
various   foundations.      "  Alms   an  nu- 
ally  distributed  to  the  poor  on  the  an- 
niversaries   of    the    deceased    persons 
undermentioned,  viz.  of  Thomas  Hat- 
felde   (Bishop  of    Durham),   40s.  ;  of 
Thomas   Castell    (Prior   of    Durham), 
40s.  ;  of  Sir  Thomas  de  Nevill,  knt., 
40s.  of  Robert  Rodes,  74s.  ;  of  Thomas 
Lawson,  Esq.,  40s.  ;  and  of  Richard  de 
Castro  Bernardi,  clerk,  40s. ;  as  appears 
in  their  charters.    And  in  alms  given  to 
the  fifteen  brethren  and  sisters  of  the 
house   of   God,   called    '  Masendewes," 
45s.     And  in  alms  paid  in  pennies  to 
four    widows  of   the    Blessed    Virgin, 
called    '  wedowwryght '  (widow-right) 
to  each   of  them,   4s.      And   in  alms 
given  and  distributed  in  money  to  the 
poor  at  the  Lord's  Supper,  and  two 
following  days,  arising  from  the  foun- 
dation   of   Absalom,    Germanus,  and 
Ralph    Karneth    (all    early    priors  of 
Durham),   £6  13s.   4d."      See   Hist. 
Dunelm.  Scrip. Tres.  App.  xxxv.  These 
distributions    were    called    maundies, 
not   from  the  maund  or  basket  out  of 
which  the  dole  was  made,  as  has  been 
frequently  suggested,  but  because  they 
were  ex  mandate,  by  express  command 
of  their  respective  founders.

Occasionally  barley  and  oats  were 
mixed  together  and  malt  made  of  the 
two  united.  "  Paid  for  1£.  qu.  of 


barley  and  oats  together,  for  malt,  10s." 
Burs.  1536. 

HAYLL  BYSCAYTTS,  230.  Qu.  ale-biscuits, 
biscuits  to  be  eaten  over  the  ale-cup. 

HAYNESTONE,  229.  As  a  razor  occurs  in 
the  next  item  this  was  probably  a  hone 
or  stone  upon  which  to  sharpen  it. 

HEDESTELLIS,  65.  Hedstells,  ornamented 
frontlets  for  bridles. 

HELTEBSHAFTES,  20.  Halter  shafts,  made 
either  of  a  thicker  rope,  or  perhaps 
occasionally  of  wood,  like  the  instru- 
ments at  present  used  by  horse-breakers 
and  farriers. 

HEWERS,  260.  Ewers,  here  made  of 
silver. 

HEYDDS  pro  sagittis,  139.     Arrow-heads. 

HOGGES.  Young  sheep,  castrated  males 
in  their  second  year. 

HOBSMET,  13.  Food  or  provender  for 
horses. 

HOSTIUM.     Ostium — a  door. 

HOURGONS,  221.  So  written,  but  per- 
haps a  mistake  for  sturgeons. 

HOYSHEB  DOYB,  170.  The  usher  door. 
The  door  leading  from  the  south-east 
corner  of  the  Cloister  into  the  Apart- 
ments of  the  Prior,  now  the  Deanery. 


J. 


JAKKS,  139.  Jacks,  body  armour  so 
called,  consisting  of  skins  of  animals 
covered  with  plates  of  steel.  Occa- 
sionally the  jack  was  made  of  two  skins 
with  a  stuffing  of  wool  between  them. 


K. 


KELYNGS,  KELYNGES,  111,  &c.  Accord- 
ing to  Jamieson,  the  name  given  by 
fishermen  to  cod  fish  of  a  large  size,  the 
younger  being  called  codlings,  but 
from  an  authority  quoted  by  him  in  his 
Supplement  it  may  be  doubted  whether 
the  keeling  is  not  in  reality  a  had- 
dock. 

KENDALL,  192.  Cloth  made  at  Kendall— 
Kendall  green  was  a  cloth  of  great 
notoriety. 

KIRTELLA,  9.  Here  a  short  upper  gar- 
ment, ornamented  with  fur,  and  worn 
by  a  gentleman.  The  word  is  still  in 
use,  but  it  is  now  applied  to  a  loose 
upper  garment  worn  by  labourers. 

KYE,  29,  327.     Cows. 

KYPSTRYNGES,  20.  Qu.  keep-strings,  a 
term  which  might  be  applied  to  cords 
for  tethering  cattle.

LADESADDILIS.  Load-saddles.  A  saddle

on  its  back.

quarts. 

LAN  DM  ALE,  101.  A  reserved  rent  charged 
upon  land  by  the  chief  lord  of  the  fee, 
or  a  subsequent  mesne  owner. 

LASSERACIO,  85.  For  laceratio.  The 
splitting  of  timber  into  spars  or  laths 
by  wedges  or  other  means. 

LATBIIODDS,  72.     Nails  for  laths. 

LATOMUS,  130.  A  stone  cutter  or  mason. 
Gr.  \aas  and  Ttp-vat. 

LEDNALLS,  LEYDNAYLES,  72,  78.  Nails 
for  lead — for  affixing  sheet-lead  to  the 
roofs  of  churches  or  houses. 

LENTES,  9,  &c.  Probably  here  oatmeal, 
from  the  oats  which  were  purchased 
and  dried  for  the  purpose. 

LESEYNG,  167,  263,  264.  This  word  is  in 
use  in  the  N.  R.  Yorks.  to  denote  the 
separating  of  noxious  weeds  from 
sheaves  intended  to  be  thrashed  for 
seed.  In  the  instances  in  which  it  oc- 
curs in  this  book,  it  is  applied  to  rye, 
and  it  may  probably  denote  the  sepa- 
rating of  rye  from  wheat,  that  the  latter 
may  be  unmixed  with  other  grain  when 
sown.  Jamieson  states  that  in  England 
to  lease  signified  to  glean,  but  it  is  not 
used  in  the  northern  counties  in  that 
sense. 

LETTYNGE,  268.  Probably  the  leading  of 
glass  in  windows. 

LEWKYNGE,  127.  The  eradicating  of 
weeds  from  corn  before  it  comes  into 
ear.  A  word  still  in  common  use. 

LEYDYRS,  270.    Ladders. 

LEYPYS,  256.  Baskets  used  in  sowing 
corn.  Hod.  hoppers. 

LIDERATURA.  Cloth  delivered  to  retainers 
and  servants  as  part  of  their  wages. 
In  the  above  accounts  the  word  is 
chiefly  used  in  this  sense,  but  it  had 
at  the  same  period  a  more  extended 
meaning,  implying  whatever  was  de- 
livered to  persons  so  situated  in  part 
of  payment. 

LICHINUS.  Candle-wicks,  of  which  the 
Bursar  purchased  considerable  quan- 
tities to  be  used  in  his  home-made 
candles. 


LOKKM  LA.VK  Small  quantities,  harxi- 
fuls,  of  wool  for  stuffing  pack-saddle*. 
But  see  the  FiathaU  Uluuary.

ferred to,  as  much  lead  an  could  be

LUDI  PRIORIS,  9,  13,  &c.  At  four  pe- 
riods of  the  year,  at  the  Feast  of  the 
Purification,  at  Easter,  on  the  day  of 
St.  John  the  Baptist,  and  on  the  day  of 
All  Saints,  the  Prior  withdrew  from 
Durham  to  one  of  his  Manor  houses, 
attended  by  his  officers  and  a  consider- 
able number  of  the  fraternity,  for  the 
purposes  of  feasting  and  relaxation. 
The  places  to  which  he  retired  in  their 
turns  were  generally  Bearpark,  Byew- 
ley,  Pittington,  and  Ward  ley,  but  Mug- 
gleswick  and  other  places  were  perhaps 
occasionally  visited,  and,  in  some  in- 
stances, after  the  ordinary  Ludtu  had 
been  brought  to  a  termination,  special 
Ludi  were  held,  which,  as*  in  the  case  at 
Byewley,  in  1538,  hereafter  mentioned, 
lasted  for  three  days  in  succession.  If 
any  opinion  can  be  formed  from  the 
consumption  of  provisions  on  these 
occasions,  the  people  of  the  neigh- 
bourhood seem  to  have  attended  in 
great  numbers,  and  in  all  probability 
one  of  the  amusements  which,  they 
were  permitted  to  witness  was  an  exhi- 
bition of  miracle  plays  or  mysteries. 
The  word  Ludus  seems  to  justify  thi* 
inference.  The  ordinary  allowance  of 
provisions  for  these  games  made  by  the 
Bursar  in  each  year  is  detailed  in  the 
pages  above  referred  to,  but  the  later 
books  afford  much  additional  informa- 
tion. In  1538,  the  Ludus  of  All  Saints 
was  held  at  Beaurepaire,  and  that  of 
St.  John  the  Baptist  preceding  at 
Byewley.  Of  the  expenditure  and 
consumption  of  food  at  this  latter  place, 
we  have  the  following  minute  account. 
It  will  be  observed  that  the  ordinary 
Ludus  is  first  mentioned.  '

Alexander  Durham,  celebrating  their

in  1538  were  Thomas  Forster,  Roger 
Raw,  John  Sotheron,  and  Richard 
Forster.

ODLAMES,  59.  In  the  page  referred  to, 
the  tithe-gatherer  is  paid  an  additional

of  these  one  would  be  due  as  a  tithe,  but 
the  trouble  of  the  collector  would  be 
materially  increased  in  gathering  these 
single  lambs  together.

PANNUS  GENEROSORUM,  VALECTOBUM, 
GROMORUM.  Cloth  or  livery  of  a  su- 
periorquality,  distributed  yearly  among 
the  yenerosi,  or  gentlemen  who  held 
office  in  the  convent.  The  valecti  or 
yeomen  who  were  next  in  rank  received 
cloth  of  an  inferior  character,  and  the 
gromi,  or  common  servants,  were  still 
more  humbly  clothed.

its  limits.

PARITER.  This  word,  when  applied,  as

were  respectively  mixed  together  in 
equal  quantities.

POWNDEK,  329.  The  Pindar.  In  a  similar

PROVISAR  Abbathiae,  or  Pro  visor,  39.

PTISINA,  5,  58,  63,  245.  This  word  has 
generally  been  considered  to  denote 
barley  water,  a  decoction  made  from 
decorticated  barley.  The  word  itself 
is  of  Greek  origin,  TrTuravr),  from 
irTicr(rG>,to  deprive  any  thing  of  its  husk 
or  shell ;  but,  as  it  was  used  at  Durham 
at  festivals  in  great  quantities,  costing 
not  less  than  4s.  Qd.  per  barrel, 
something  stronger  than  barley  water 
must  be  denoted.  It  was  probably  ale 
of  a  weaker  kind  for  the  populace.  To 
the  notices  of  this  beverage,  whatever 
it  was,  referred  to,  the  following  may  be 
added :  "  Four  barrels  of  ptisan  bought 


of  Nicholas  Newsham.  16*."—  1536, 
"  Paid  to  William  Lame  and  two  assist- 
ants for  carrying  three  loads  of  ptisan 
from  the  Monastery  to  Shincliffe  moor 
on  the  road  to  Bewley  2s." — 1538.  In 
the  same  year,  "  Paid  to  the  tenants  of 
Shincliffe  as  a  present  for  the  carriage 
of  two  tuns  of  ptisan .  (the  same  doubt- 
less as  above)  to  Quarrington  moor, 
12e£."  Shincliffe  moor  and  Quarring- 
ton moor  are  identical,  and  they  are  on 
the  road  from  Durham  to  Bewley,  at 
which  place  the  Prior  was  then  holding 
his  Ludi,  and  where  barley  water  might 
have  been  easily  made,  and  the  expense 
of  carrying  it  from  Durham  saved. 
"  In  two  barrels  of  ptisan  bought  of 
Nicholas  Newsham  against  Christmas, 
9«."— 1539.  y.  Hor.  Sat.  ii.  3.  155. 
Ptisan  is  there  made  of  rice.  Since  the 
above  memoranda  were  committed  to 
paper  an  entry  in  the  Bursar's  Book  for 
the  year  1539-40  has  been  met  with 
under  the  head  of  "  Halmot  Courts," 
which  seems  to  remove  all  doubt  with 
respect  to  the  precise  meaning  of  the 
word  under  notice.  In  the  second 
turn  the  steward  purchases,  among 
other  things,  for  his  court-dinner, 
ptisan  5s.  and  ale  2s.  4c£.  (ptissina  & 
cervisia).  In  the  third  turn  no  men- 
tion is  made  of  ptisina  in  his  dinner 
bill,  but  we  have  in  plain  English  from 
the  same  person  of  whom  on  the  for- 
mer occasion  the  ptisan  was  brought, 
"  1  barell  syngyll  beyr  and  1  fyrkynge 
doubyll  beayre  4s.  id."  which  strongly 
confirms  the  conjecture  above  hazarded, 
and  appears  to  settle  the  question.  See 
Halmotti  above.

implies,  was  for  the  purpose  of  making 
nets  or  springes  for  wood-cocks. 

TWYNTERS,  205.     Cattle  two  winters  old. 


U.  V. 

UNCCIO,  157.  Unctio  Ovium,  the  anoint- 
ing or  greasing  of  sheep  before  they 
they  are  shorn. 

UNDERCLOTHES,  157.  Table  cloths, 
probably  of  a  coarser  description, 
used  beneath  others  of  more  value. 

URE,  47.     Lead-ore. 

VALECTUS.  A  valet  or  yeoman,  a  do- 
mestic ranking  between  the  generosi 
or  gentlemen  and  the  gromi  or  com- 
mon servants.  The  Valectus  Promp- 
tuarii  is,  in  1517,  the  butler.  The 


Bursar,  when  he  speaks  of  the  Valecti 
in  English,  calls  them  yeomen.  "  1508. 
Hugh  Holland,  *  sewer,'  the  *  yeoman 
usher,'  the  *  yeoman  butler.' "  The 
gromus  or  lower  menial  remains  un- 
translated. 

VANGA,  74.  A  wooden  shovel.  An 
early  printed  book  of  Homilies,  in 
the  library  of  the  Dean  and  Chapter 
of  Durham,  contains,  in  the  hand-writ- 
ing of  the  Monk  William  Law,  once 
its  owner,  the  following  distich,  which 
comprises  the  names  of  many  of  these 
rustic  implements. — Liber  Loquitur. 
"  Non  manibus  duris  me  comprimat 
incola  ruris  Cujus  sunt  juris,  traha, 
tribula,  vanga,  securis." 

VECTURA  FRATRUM,  71, 102.  The  Travelr 
ling  expenses  of  the  monks  when  re- 
moving from  one  cell  to  another,  or 
to  or  from  the  monastery  at  home. 
"Paid  to  John  Ereston,  11  Sep.  going 
towards  Fame,  5s.  To  William  Hartyl- 
pole,  10  Jul.  going  to  Letham,  10s. 
To  John  Balyer  19  May,  going  to 
Letham,  10s. — 1517.  Bursar's  Book. 

VERIDARIUS.     For  Veredarius,  a  carter. 

VERINT,  30, 108. 

VIRG^J  86.  Twigs  used  in  thatching  and 
stoothing. 

VOLATILES.     Fowls,  poultry. 

VOLSKYNGS,  305.  The  page  referred  to 
proves  that  the  name  volskyng,  or 
wool-skin,  was  applied  to  such  sheep 
as  were  slaughtered  between  Michael- 
mas and  the  beginning  of  Lent. 

VRYCLOTHE,  157.

for  the  timber  to  rest  upon.

WODYRE,  24,  191,  297.  The  payment 
hero  made  is  stated  in  the  Book  for 
1507,  to  be  for  wodhire,  the  word 
being  written  at  length,  in  1509  wode- 
hyr,  in  1510  wodhire,  in  1517  wodyre, 
all  probably  denoting  the  hire  or  out- 
rent  of  the  wood.  In  this  our  Book  the 
word  is  never  written  at  length,  and  in 
1538  and  1539  it  is  called  wpdyeron, 
apparently  by  mistake.  See  the  next 
word.

YANK,  76, 127.  One.  It  is  here  a  har- 
vest term.  Three  reaper*,  generally 
women,  with  a  man  to  bind  behind 
them,  constitute  a  yane  (the  term  is  still 
in  general  use)  or  one — i.e.  the  com- 
plement for  one  ridge,  and  a  farmer  is 
said  to  have  so  many  yane  at  work  as  he 
has  ridges  reaped  at  the  same  time.
